The shifted ODE method for underdamped Langevin MCMC
James Foster, Terry Lyons, Harald Oberhauser

Abstract
In this paper, we consider the underdamped Langevin diffusion (ULD) and propose a numerical approximation using its associated ordinary differential equation (ODE). When used as a Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithm, we show that the ODE approximation achieves a 2 2\hskip 0.7113pt -Wasserstein error of ε \varepsilon in 𝒪 ⁡ ( d 3 / ε 2 3 ) \mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt[3]{d\hskip 0.7113pt}/\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{3}}\big) steps under the standard smoothness and strong convexity assumptions on the target distribution. This matches the complexity of the randomized midpoint method proposed by Shen and Lee [NeurIPS 2019] which was shown to be order optimal by Cao, Lu and Wang. However, the main feature of the proposed numerical method is that it can utilize additional smoothness of the target log-density f f . More concretely, we show that the ODE approximation achieves a 2 2\hskip 0.7113pt -Wasserstein error of ε \varepsilon in 𝒪 ⁡ ( d 2 5 / ε 2 5 ) \mathcal{O}\big(d^{\frac{2}{5}}/\varepsilon^{\frac{2}{5}}\big) and 𝒪 ⁡ ( d / ε 1 3 ) \mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}/\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{3}}\big) steps when Lipschitz continuity is assumed for the Hessian and third derivative of f f . By discretizing this ODE using a third order Runge-Kutta method, we can obtain a practical MCMC method that uses just two additional gradient evaluations per step. In our experiment, where the target comes from a logistic regression, this method shows faster convergence compared to other unadjusted Langevin MCMC algorithms.



1
Introduction

Traditionally used to model coarse-grained particle systems
[
34
,
38
,
14
]
, the underdamped Langevin diffusion (ULD) is given by the following stochastic differential equation (SDE):

\displaystyle dx_{t}

\displaystyle=v_{t}\,dt,

(1.1)

\displaystyle dv_{t}

\displaystyle=-\gamma v_{t}\,dt-u\nabla f(x_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\,dW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

where
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represent the position and momentum of a particle,

f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes a scalar potential,
\gamma>0
is a friction coefficient and
W
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion.

Under mild assumptions, it is known that (
1.1
) admits a unique strong solution which is ergodic with respect to the Gibbs measure with Hamiltonian
H(x,v)=f(x)+\frac{1}{2u}\|v\|^{2}

(we refer the reader to Proposition 6.1 in
[
50
]
for further details). That is, the diffusion process has a stationary measure
\pi
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with density proportional to
\exp(-H(x,v))
.
In particular, the (marginal) stationary distribution for
x
is proportional to
\exp(-f(x))

and thus solving (
1.1
) is a method of sampling from unnormalized log-concave densities.
When the SDE solution cannot be obtained exactly, it must be appropriately discretized
and so numerical methods are required to apply ULD to log-concave sampling problems.
In this paper, we develop an ordinary differential equation (ODE) based method for ULD.

Langevin-type equations are widely used in statistical physics as they are natural extensions of Hamiltonian systems that can model randomness
[
14
,
46
]
. More recently, they have been applied to sampling and optimization problems in machine learning
[
56
,
44
,
43
]
.
As a result, the study of (underdamped) Langevin dynamics is an active area of research
with an extensive literature. Below we give a brief overview for some of this related work:

Numerical methods for underdamped Langevin dynamics

A variety of approximations have been proposed for ULD. Some noteworthy examples are
the unadjusted and Metropolis-adjusted OBABO schemes
[
4
,
47
,
54
]
, the randomized midpoint method
[
52
,
31
]
(which was shown to be order optimal in
[
8
]
), the left-point
1
1

1

In
[
12
]
, the proposed approximation is referred to as the “discrete underdamped Langevin diffusion”. We shall refer to this approach as the “left-point method” (simply to distinguish it from other methods).
method
[
12
,
16
,
11
]
, Strang splitting
[
3
,
35
]
and quasi-symplectic Runge-Kutta methods
[
45
]
.
We shall compare our approach to several of these methods in our numerical experiment.

Probabilistic couplings for underdamped Langevin dynamics

The analysis of ULD and its discretizations often makes use of probabilistic couplings.
In
[
22
]
, the authors used reflection and synchronous couplings to establish a (Wasserstein) contraction property for the diffusion. Similar contraction theorems were later obtained in
[
12
,
16
]
where only synchronous couplings were used. The exponential contractivity of Langevin dynamics allows one to obtain non-asymptotic estimates between the diffusion and approximation processes directly from local mean and mean-square estimates
[
39
]
.
Contraction results have also been established for the OBABO discretization of (
1.1
) using synchronous and Gaussian couplings
[
47
]
. However, unlike in
[
39
]
, the author of
[
47
]
obtains non-asymptotic estimates without comparing to the SDE solution (instead the OBABO chain is compared to another Markov chain with the desired stationary measure).
In
[
19
]
, the authors propose the use of couplings to empirically estimate the convergence of a numerical solution to the stationary measure of an ergodic SDE. For example, they use a combination of reflection, synchronous and maximal couplings to estimate the convergence of an Euler-Maruyama discretization of ULD with nonconvex potential
f(x)=\big(\|x\|_{2}^{2}-1\big)^{2}
.

Applications of Langevin dynamics to MCMC algorithms and optimization

After the breakthrough papers of Neal
[
49
]
along with Girolami and Calderhead
[
30
]
, there has significant interest in Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) methods for sampling and integral estimation. Since these MCMC methods are based on Hamiltonian systems, they are intimately connected to Langevin dynamics
[
21
,
43
,
10
,
54
,
2
]
. More recently, the Metropolis-adjusted OBABO sampler (an MCMC algorithm which converges to ULD
[
47
]
)
was shown to empirically outperform the standard HMC method
[
54
]
. Alongside MCMC
[
18
,
54
,
55
]
,
underdamped Langevin diffusions have also been considered for optimization due to their natural parallels with existing momentum-based algorithms
[
28
,
29
,
42
,
36
,
53
]
.

1.1. Contributions

Our key contribution is an ODE-based numerical method for ULD, which we refer to as the “shifted ODE method”. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first derivative-free approximation of ULD that can achieve third order convergence
(a third order numerical method is given in
[
45
]
but it requires further derivatives of
\nabla f
).

Definition 1.1

(
Shifted ODE method
)
.

Let
\{t_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
be a sequence of times with
t_{0}=0
,

t_{n+1}>t_{n}
and step sizes
h_{n}=t_{n+1}-t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
, for
n\geq 0
. We construct a numerical solution

\{(\widetilde{x}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
by setting
(\widetilde{x}_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{0}):=(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})

and for each
n\geq 0
, defining
(\widetilde{x}_{n+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n+1})
as

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widetilde{x}_{n+1}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widetilde{v}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\Bigg):=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}_{1}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\overline{v}_{1}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)-\big(H_{n}-6K_{n}\big)\Bigg(\begin{matrix}0\\[-3.0pt]
\sqrt{2\gamma u}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

where
\{(\overline{x}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn},\overline{v}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn})\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\in\hskip 0.7113pt[0,1]}
solves the following (rescaled) Langevin-type ODE,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-4.0pt]
\,\overline{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-4.0pt]
\,-\gamma\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}-u\nabla f\big(\,\overline{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Bigg(\begin{matrix}0\\[-4.0pt]
\sqrt{2\gamma u}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

(1.2)

with initial condition

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}_{0}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\overline{v}_{0}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg):=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widetilde{x}_{n}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widetilde{v}_{n}\end{matrix}\Bigg)+\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\Bigg(\begin{matrix}0\\[-3.0pt]
\sqrt{2\gamma u}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

and the random Gaussian vectors
\{W_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\hskip 0.7113pt,\,\{H_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
and
\{K_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
are independent with

W_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,h_{n}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt,\,H_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\frac{1}{12}h_{n}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\big)
and
K_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\frac{1}{720}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\big)
for all
n\geq 0
. Moreover,
we define
\{\hskip 0.7113ptW_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptH_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113ptK_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt\}
from the same Brownian motion
W
(see Theorem
3.5
for details).

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle:=W_{t_{n+1}}-W_{t_{n}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle H_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h_{n}}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\Big(\big(W_{t}-W_{t_{n}}\big)-\frac{t-t_{n}}{h_{n}}\hskip 0.7113ptW_{n}\Big)\,dt,

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\bigg(\frac{1}{2}h_{n}-(t-t_{n})\bigg)\Big(\big(W_{t}-W_{t_{n+1}}\big)-\frac{t-t_{n}}{h_{n}}\hskip 0.7113ptW_{n}\Big)\,dt.

We discuss the intuition behind this method in Section
3
and perform an error analysis in the appendices. In Table
1.1
, we compare this ODE approach to some related methods:

method

[

\ast

We expect these complexities to be reduced when the ODE is discretized using standard high order ODE solvers. In this case, we expect the complexities to be
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}/\varepsilon\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}/\hskip-0.35565pt\sqrt{\varepsilon}\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
respectively.
On the other hand, if a randomized ODE solver is applied (such as a randomized midpoint method),
we conjecture that it is possible to achieve these complexities. This may be a topic for future work.

\dagger

These orders are conjectured, but seem quite likely given the method’s stochastic Taylor expansion.

In practice, the ODE (
1.2
) cannot be solved exactly, so must be discretized using an appropriate ODE solver. For the resulting method to demonstrate third order convergence, we expect (but have not proven) that it suffices to use a third order numerical ODE solver.
Therefore we consider two approaches which are derived from the standard third order Runge-Kutta method
[
5
]
and the fourth order splitting method of Forest and Ruth
[
23
]
.
In order to distinguish them, we refer to these discretizations of ULD as the SORT
2
2

2

S
hifted
O
DE with
R
unge-Kutta-
T
hree.
and SOFA
3
3

3

S
hifted
O
DE with
F
ourth-order-splitting
A
pplied.
methods. Due to the structure of the “shifted ODE”, the SORT and SOFA methods only require two and three additional evaluations of
\nabla f
per step respectively.
This means the SORT and SOFA methods have similar cost to the methods in Table
1.1
,
which use one extra evaluation per step (except the randomized midpoint which uses two).
However by using more evaluations of
\nabla f
per step, the SORT and SOFA methods can exhibit faster
convergence when
\nabla f
has more smoothness (see the numerical experiment).

Definition 1.2

(
The SORT method
)
.

Let
\{t_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
be a sequence of times with
t_{0}=0
,

t_{n+1}>t_{n}
and step sizes
h_{n}=t_{n+1}-t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
. We construct a numerical solution

\{(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
for (
1.1
) by setting
\hskip 0.7113pt(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0})\hskip 0.7113pt:=\hskip 0.7113pt(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})\hskip 0.7113pt

and for
n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0
, defining
\hskip 0.7113pt(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\hskip 0.7113pt)\hskip 0.7113pt
as follows:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big),

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}}}{\gamma}\bigg)\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}-\bigg(\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}-1}{\gamma^{2}}\bigg)u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 31.29802pt+\bigg(\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}-1}{\gamma^{2}h_{n}}\bigg)\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma h_{n}}}{\gamma}\bigg)\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}-\bigg(\frac{e^{-\gamma h_{n}}+\gamma h_{n}-1}{\gamma^{2}}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{3}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)+\frac{2}{3}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 31.29802pt+\bigg(\frac{e^{-\gamma h_{n}}+\gamma h_{n}-1}{\gamma^{2}h_{n}}\bigg)\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}

\displaystyle:=e^{-\gamma h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}-\frac{1}{6}\hskip 0.7113pte^{-\gamma h_{n}}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}-\frac{2}{3}\hskip 0.7113pte^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma h_{n}}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)h_{n}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)h_{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 31.29802pt+\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma h_{n}}}{\gamma h_{n}}\bigg)\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big),

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}-\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(H_{n}-6K_{n}\big),

where
\{W_{n},H_{n},K_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
are the Gaussian random vectors given in definition
1.1
.

Definition 1.3

(
The SOFA method
)
.

Let
\{t_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
be a sequence of times with
t_{0}=0
,

t_{n+1}>t_{n}
and step sizes
h_{n}=t_{n+1}-t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
. We construct a numerical solution

\{(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
for (
1.1
) by setting
\hskip 0.7113pt(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0})\hskip 0.7113pt:=\hskip 0.7113pt(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})\hskip 0.7113pt

and for
n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0
, defining
\hskip 0.7113pt(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\hskip 0.7113pt)\hskip 0.7113pt
as follows:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(0)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big),

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=e^{-\gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\phi)h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(0)}+\Big(\hskip-0.7113pt-u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Big)\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\phi\right)h_{n}}}{\gamma h_{n}}\bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big(1+2\phi\big)h_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}

\displaystyle:=e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}+\Big(\hskip-0.7113pt-u\nabla f\hskip-1.42262pt\left(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\right)\hskip-0.35565pth_{n}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Big)\bigg(\frac{1-e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}}{\gamma h_{n}}\bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}-\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\big(1+4\phi\big)h_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(3)}

\displaystyle:=e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}+\Big(\hskip-0.7113pt-u\nabla f\hskip-1.42262pt\left(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\right)\hskip-0.35565pth_{n}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Big)\bigg(\frac{1-e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}}{\gamma h_{n}}\bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}+\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(3)}\big(1+2\phi\big)h_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(4)}

\displaystyle:=e^{-\gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\phi)h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(3)}+\Big(\hskip-0.7113pt-u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\sqrt{2\gamma u}\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Big)\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\phi\right)h_{n}}}{\gamma h_{n}}\bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(4)}-\sqrt{2\gamma u}\hskip 0.7113pt\big(H_{n}-6K_{n}\big),

where
\{W_{n},H_{n},K_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
are the Gaussian vectors from definition
1.1
and
\phi
is given by

\displaystyle\phi:=\frac{-1+\sqrt[3]{2\hskip 0.7113pt}\,}{2\big(2-\sqrt[3]{2\hskip 0.7113pt}\,\big)}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Despite being straightforward to implement, the above methods are difficult to analyse.
At the very least, it should be much easier to study the shifted ODE method due to the similarities in structure between the SDE (
1.1
) and the ODE (
1.2
). For example, both differential equations have continuous-time solutions and admit similar Taylor expansions.
As our analysis is based on these continuous-time Taylor expansions, it does not apply if the ODE is discretized. That said, we expect these methods to achieve a
2
-Wasserstein error of
\varepsilon
in
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}/\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{k}}\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
steps when the first
k
derivatives of
f
are Lipschitz continuous.
In addition, we do not study the numerical stability of the SORT and SOFA methods.
Therefore it is likely that further improvements can be made to the proposed ODE solvers (particularly for large step sizes). For example, it may be possible to discretize (
1.2
) using ideas from
[
6
,
7
]
, where the stability of splitting methods is considered in the HMC setting.

Although this is not explored in the paper, it is also straightforward to incorporate adaptive step sizes into these methodologies. That is, given
(W_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,H_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,K_{n})
, we can generate the same triple over the intervals
[t_{n},t_{n+\frac{1}{2}}]
and
[t_{n+\frac{1}{2}},t_{n+1}]
where
t_{n+\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{1}{2}(t_{n}+t_{n+1})
.
This procedure can be viewed as an extension of Lévy’s construction of Brownian motion and is detailed in
[
24
]
. We expect that adaptive step sizes are also possible for the other ULD numerical methods (given in section
4
). However this may be a topic for future work.

1.2. Organization of the paper

Having introduced our proposed numerical methods, we now outline the paper.
In Section
2
we establish our key notation, definitions and assumptions. In Section
3
we give an informal derivation of the shifted ODE method and present our main results (which imply the
2
-Wasserstein complexities given in Table
1.1
).
In Section
4
, we discuss some related methods for ULD in preparation of our numerical experiment. In Section
5
, we empirically compare the SORT and SOFA methods to these related methods in an example where the target density comes from a logistic regression.
The technical details for our analysis of the shifted ODE method are given in the appendix.

2
Notation and definitions

In this section, we present some of the notation, definitions and assumptions for the paper.

Throughout, we use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Along with the Euclidean norm, we use the usual inner product
\langle\hskip 0.7113pt\cdot\hskip 0.7113pt,\hskip 0.7113pt\cdot\hskip 0.7113pt\rangle
with
\langle v,v\rangle=\|v\|_{2}^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt
.
For a function
g
, we use Big O notation
O(g)
and let
\mathcal{O}(g)
be the class
O(g)\cdot(\log(g))^{\hskip 0.7113ptO(1)}
.

2.1. Assumptions on
f

The function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is assumed to be
m
-strongly convex

\displaystyle f(y)\geq f(x)+\big\langle\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla f(x),y-x\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}m\|x-y\|_{2}^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.1)

and at least twice continuously differentiable with an
M
-Lipschitz continuous gradient
\nabla f\hskip-0.35565pt
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\|_{2}\leq M\|x-y\|_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.2)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is straightforward to show that the conditions (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) are equivalent to the Hessian of
f
being positive definite and satisfying
mI_{d}\preccurlyeq\nabla^{2}f(x)\preccurlyeq MI_{d}\hskip 0.7113pt
(where
I_{d}
is the
d\times d
identity matrix and
A\preccurlyeq B
means that
x^{\mathsf{T}}\hskip-2.84526ptA\hskip 0.7113ptx\leq x^{\mathsf{T}}\hskip-0.7113ptB\hskip 0.7113ptx
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\hskip 0.7113pt
).

At certain points in our analysis, we make additional smoothness assumptions on
f
.
Before we give these assumptions, we first recall the notions of tensors and operator norms.
For
k\geq 1
, a
k
-tensor on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is simply an element of the
d^{\hskip 0.7113ptk}
-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{d\times\cdots\times d}
.
We can interpret a
k
-tensor on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as a multilinear map into
\mathbb{R}
with
k
arguments from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Hence for a
k
-tensor
T
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(with
k\geq 2
) and a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define
Tv:=T(v,\cdots)
as a
(k-1)
-tensor on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Using this, we can define the operator norm of
T
recursively as

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\text{op}}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\,\,\,\hskip 0.7113pt\|T\|_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,&\text{if}\,\,T\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.0pt]
\,\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}v\hskip 0.7113pt\in\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[2.0pt]
\|v\|_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\leq\hskip 0.7113pt1\end{subarray}}{\sup}\|Tv\|_{\text{op}}\hskip 0.7113pt,&\text{if}\,\,T\,\,\text{is a }k\text{-tensor on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ with }k\geq 2\hskip 0.7113pt.\end{cases}

With a slight abuse of notation, we will write
\|\cdot\|_{2}
instead of
\|\cdot\|_{\text{op}}
for all
k
-tensors.
In Section
D.1
, we additionally assume that the Hessian of
f
is
M_{2}
-Lipschitz continuous:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}f(x)-\nabla^{2}f(y)\right\|_{2}\leq M_{2}\|x-y\|_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.3)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. In Section
D.2
, we shall additionally assume that the function
f
is three times continuously differentiable and its third derivative is
M_{3}
-Lipschitz continuous:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{3}f(x)-\nabla^{3}f(y)\right\|_{2}\leq M_{2}\|x-y\|_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.4)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
.

2.2. Probability notation

Suppose
\big(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\,;\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\big)
is a filtered probability space carrying a standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion. The only SDE that we analyse in this paper is (
1.1
), which admits a unique strong solution that is ergodic and whose stationary measure
\pi
has a density
\pi(x,v)\propto e^{-f(x)+\frac{1}{2u}\|v\|_{2}^{2}}
under our assumptions on
f
(see Proposition 6.1 in
[
50
]
). Numerical SDE solutions are obtained at times
\{t_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
with
t_{0}=0
,
t_{n+1}>t_{n}
and step sizes
h_{n}=t_{n+1}-t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
. The results in Table
1.1
assume fixed steps.
Given a random variable
X
, taking its values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we define the
\mathbb{L}_{p}
norm of
X
as

\displaystyle\|X\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\|X\|_{2}^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}},

for
p\geq 1
. Similarly, for a stochastic process
\{X_{t}\}
, the
\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}
-conditional
\mathbb{L}_{p}
norm of
X_{t}
is

\displaystyle\|X_{t}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}^{n}}:=\mathbb{E}_{n}\Big[\|X_{t}\|_{2}^{p}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\|X_{t}\|_{2}^{p}\,\big|\,\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p}},

for
t\geq t_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt
. For
0\leq s\leq t
, we use
X_{s,t}:=X_{t}-X_{s}
to denote the increment of
X
over
[s,t]
.

2.3. Coupling and Wasserstein distance

Let
\mu
,
\nu
be probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
A coupling between
\mu
and
\nu
is a random variable
Z=(X,Y)
for which
X\sim\mu
and
Y\sim\nu
.
For
p\geq 1
, we define the
p
-Wasserstein distance between
\mu
and
\nu
as

\displaystyle W_{p}\big(\mu,\nu\big):=\inf_{(X,Y)\hskip 0.7113pt\sim\hskip 0.7113pt(\mu\times\nu)}\|X-Y\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.5)

where the above infimum is taken over all couplings of
random variables
X
and
Y
with distributions
X\sim\mu
and
Y\sim\nu
. In this paper, we obtain non-asymptotic bounds for the
2
-Wasserstein distance between the target distribution and shifted ODE approximation.

2.4. Diffusion and approximation processes

We shall use
W
to denote a standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion and define the (piecewise) Brownian bridge process
B
as

\displaystyle B_{t}=W_{t}-\bigg(W_{t_{n}}+\bigg(\frac{t-t_{n}}{h_{n}}\bigg)\hskip 0.7113ptW_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\bigg),

(2.6)

for
t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
and
n\geq 0
. Using
W
and
B
, we can define the following Gaussian vectors:

\displaystyle W_{n}

\displaystyle:=W_{t_{n},t_{n+1}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(2.7)

\displaystyle H_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h_{n}}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\hskip-2.84526ptB_{t_{n},t}\,dt,

(2.8)

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h_{n}^{2}}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\bigg(\frac{1}{2}h_{n}-(t-t_{n})\bigg)B_{t_{n},t}\,dt.

(2.9)

Since “
\hskip 0.7113pt12K_{n}
” appears in the shifted ODE, we also consider the “shifted” Brownian bridge:

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{t}:=B_{t}+12K_{n}\bigg(\frac{t-t_{n}}{h_{n}}\bigg),

for
t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1})
and
n\geq 0
. In the SDE (
1.1
), we use
\sigma:=\sqrt{2\gamma u}
to simply the notation. In our analysis of ULD, we shall use the following diffusion and approximation processes:

1. The underdamped Langevin diffusion
\{(x_{t},v_{t})\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
is defined by the SDE,

\displaystyle dx_{t}

\displaystyle=v_{t}\,dt,

\displaystyle dv_{t}

\displaystyle=-\gamma v_{t}\,dt-u\nabla f(x_{t})\,dt+\sigma\,dW_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

with initial condition
(x_{0},v_{0})\sim p_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt
, where
p_{0}
is a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. We usually set
p_{0}
to the unique invariant measure
\pi
of ULD, which has density
\pi(x,v)\propto e^{-f(x)+\frac{1}{2u}\|v\|_{2}^{2}}
.
In this case, we have
(x_{t},v_{t})\sim\pi
for all
t\geq 0\hskip 0.7113pt
, and so the
2
-Wasserstein distance between
(the law of) an approximation
\widetilde{x}_{n}
at time
t_{n}
and
\pi
can be estimated using
\|\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}
.

2. Instead of using definition
1.1
, it will be more convenient to define the shifted ODE approximation
\{(\widetilde{x}_{n},\widetilde{v}_{n})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
by setting
(\widetilde{x}_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{0})\sim\widetilde{p}_{0}
(for some distribution
\widetilde{p}_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}\hskip 0.7113pt
) and defining
(\widetilde{x}_{n+1},\widetilde{v}_{n+1})
as

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widetilde{x}_{n+1}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widetilde{v}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\Bigg):=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{x}_{t_{n+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widehat{v}_{t_{n+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)+12K_{n}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

where
\big\{\big(\widehat{x}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn},\widehat{v}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\big\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\in\hskip 0.7113pt[t_{n},t_{n+1}]}
solves the following ODE,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,-\gamma\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\big)-u\nabla f\big(\,\widehat{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)+\frac{W_{n}-12K_{n}}{h_{n}}\,\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

with initial condition
\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\widehat{x}_{t_{n}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn},\widehat{v}_{t_{n}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big):=\big(\widetilde{x}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)
.

3. We also consider a sequence of shifted ODE approximations
\{(x_{n}^{\prime},v_{n}^{\prime})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
given by

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,x_{n+1}^{\prime}\\[-3.0pt]
\,v_{n+1}^{\prime}\end{matrix}\Bigg):=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\wideparen{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t_{n+1}}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t_{n+1}}\end{matrix}\Bigg)+12K_{n}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}0\\[-3.0pt]
\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

where
\big\{\big(\wideparen{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t},\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t}\big)\big\}_{t\in[t_{n},t_{n+1}]}
solves the following ODE,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\wideparen{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,-\gamma\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\big)-u\nabla f\big(\,\wideparen{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)+\frac{W_{n}-12K_{n}}{h_{n}}\,\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

with initial condition
\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\wideparen{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t_{n}}\hskip 0.7113pt,\wideparen{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}_{t_{n}}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt:=\hskip 0.7113pt\big(x_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{n}\big)
. In addition, we set
(\hskip 0.7113ptx_{0}^{\prime}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0}^{\prime}\hskip 0.7113pt)\hskip 0.7113pt:=\hskip 0.7113pt(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})\hskip 0.7113pt\sim\hskip 0.7113ptp_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt
.

4. Since we use the same
2
-Wasserstein contractivity arguments as those given in
[
16
]
, we define certain linear combinations of
\{(x_{t_{n}},v_{t_{n}})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\hskip 0.7113pt
,
\{(\widetilde{x}_{n},\widetilde{v}_{n})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
and
\{(x_{n}^{\prime},v_{n}^{\prime})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\hskip 0.7113pt
:

\displaystyle y_{n}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)-\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)-\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)-\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}^{\prime}+v_{n}^{\prime}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)-\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}^{\prime}+v_{n}^{\prime}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg),

\displaystyle y_{n}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}^{\prime}+v_{n}^{\prime}\big)-\big(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}^{\prime}+v_{n}^{\prime}\big)-\big(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg),

where
\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2}\gamma)
and
\eta:=\gamma-\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt
.

We use the notation
\big\{\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)\big\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
to denote the other numerical solutions of ULD (SORT, SOFA, OBABO, Strang splitting, randomized midpoint and left-point methods).
ULD and its various approximations will be defined from the same Brownian motion
W
.

3
Derivation and error analysis of the shifted ODE method

In this section, we shall discuss the theoretical aspects of the shifted ODE method and present our main results regarding the
2
-Wasserstein convergence of (the law of)
\widetilde{x}_{n}
to
\pi
.
For general SDEs, it is well known that the best
\mathbb{L}_{2}
approximations converge with rate
O\big(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
where
N
is the number of Gaussian random variables used by the method
[
13
,
17
]
.
Hence we begin the section by explaining why high order methods are possible for ULD.

3.1. The stochastic Taylor expansion of ULD

Perhaps the most important tool when developing and analysing numerical methods for SDEs is the stochastic Taylor expansion.
Just like for ODEs, this allows one to express the solution as a sum of “polynomial” and “remainder” terms. The challenge for SDEs is that these “polynomial” terms can now include certain iterated integrals of Brownian motion that are difficult to generate
[
27
,
25
]
.
By applying the Itô-Taylor expansion (Theorem 5.5.1 in
[
32
]
) to the Langevin SDE (
1.1
),
we see the significant terms are either monomials in
h=t-s
or Gaussian integrals of
W
.

Theorem 3.1

(
High order Taylor expansion of ULD
)
.

Consider the SDE (
1.1
) and suppose that the potential
f
is three times continuously differentiable. Then for
0\leq s\leq t
,

\displaystyle x_{t}=x_{s}

\displaystyle+v_{s}\,h-\frac{1}{2}\big(\gamma v_{s}+u\nabla f(x_{s})\big)h^{2}+\sigma\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,r}\,dr-\sigma\gamma\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}W_{s,{r_{2}}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

(3.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}\big(\gamma^{2}v_{s}+\gamma u\nabla f(x_{s})-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}\big)h^{3}

\displaystyle+\sigma\big(\gamma^{2}-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}W_{s,r_{3}}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{24}\big(\big(\gamma^{2}-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})\big)\big(\gamma v_{s}+u\nabla f(x_{s})\big)-u\gamma\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}+u\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})(v_{s},v_{s})\big)h^{4}

\displaystyle+R^{\hskip 0.7113ptx}(h,x_{s},v_{s})\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle v_{t}=v_{s}

\displaystyle-\big(\gamma v_{s}+u\nabla f(x_{s})\big)h+\sigma W_{s,t}-\sigma\gamma\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,r}\,dr

(3.2)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\big(\gamma^{2}v_{s}+u\gamma\nabla f(x_{s})-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}\big)h^{2}+\sigma\big(\gamma^{2}-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}W_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{6}\big(\big(\gamma^{2}-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})\big)\big(\gamma v_{s}+u\nabla f(x_{s})\big)-u\gamma\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}+u\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})(v_{s},v_{s})\big)h^{3}

\displaystyle-\sigma\gamma\big(\gamma^{2}-u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}W_{s,r_{3}}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle-\sigma u\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})\big(v_{s},(r_{2}-s)W_{s,r_{2}}\big)\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}+R^{\hskip 0.7113ptv}(h,x_{s},v_{s}).

where
h=t-s
and the remainder terms
R^{\hskip 0.7113ptx}(h,x_{s},v_{s})
,
R^{\hskip 0.7113ptv}(h,x_{s},v_{s})
are given by

\displaystyle R^{\hskip 0.7113ptx}(h,x_{s},v_{s})

\displaystyle=\gamma\big(u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})-\gamma^{2}\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{3}}(v_{r_{4}}-v_{s})\,dr_{4}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+u\big(u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})-\gamma^{2}\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{3}}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla f(x_{r_{4}})-\nabla f(x_{s})\big)\,dr_{4}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+u\gamma\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{3}}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla^{2}f(x_{r_{4}})v_{r_{4}}-\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}\big)\,dr_{4}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-u\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{3}}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla^{3}f(x_{r_{4}})(v_{r_{4}},v_{r_{3}})-\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})(v_{s},v_{s})\big)\,dr_{4}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle R^{\hskip 0.7113ptv}(h,x_{s},v_{s})

\displaystyle=\gamma\big(u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})-\gamma^{2}\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\big(v_{r_{3}}-v_{s}-\sigma W_{s,r_{3}}\big)\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+u\big(u\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})-\gamma^{2}\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\big(\nabla f(x_{r_{3}})-\nabla f(x_{s})\big)\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+u\gamma\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\big(\nabla^{2}f(x_{r_{3}})v_{r_{3}}-\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{s}\big)\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-u\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla^{3}f(x_{r_{3}})(v_{r_{3}},v_{r_{2}})-\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})(v_{s},v_{s})\big)\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sigma u\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla^{3}f(x_{r_{3}})\big(v_{r_{3}},W_{s,r_{2}}\big)-\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})\big(v_{s},W_{s,r_{2}}\big)\big)\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Proof.

For
\tau\in[s,t]
, the SDE (
1.1
) can be written in integral form as

\displaystyle x_{\tau}

\displaystyle=x_{s}+\int_{s}^{\tau}v_{r}\,dr,

(3.5)

\displaystyle v_{\tau}

\displaystyle=v_{s}-\gamma\int_{s}^{\tau}v_{r}\,dr-u\int_{s}^{\tau}\nabla f(x_{r})\,dr+\sigma W_{s,\tau}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(3.6)

Moreover, it then follows by Itô’s lemma that

\displaystyle\nabla f(x_{r_{1}})

\displaystyle=\nabla f(x_{s})+\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\nabla^{2}f(x_{r_{2}})\,dx_{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\nabla f(x_{s})+\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\nabla^{2}f(x_{r_{2}})v_{r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f(x_{r_{2}})v_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{\tau}+\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\nabla^{3}f(x_{r_{3}})\big(dx_{r_{3}},v_{\tau}\big)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{2}f(x_{s})v_{\tau}+\int_{s}^{r_{2}}\nabla^{3}f(x_{r_{3}})(v_{r_{3}},v_{\tau})\,dr_{3}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(3.8)

for
s\leq r_{1}\leq r_{2}\leq t
. The result now follows by repeatedly substituting the four identities (
3.5
) – (
3.8
) into the integral equations (
3.5
) – (
3.6
) and rearranging the various terms.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

As a quick sanity check, we see that by integrating the lower order terms in the expansion of
v
over
[s,t]
, we obtain the corresponding terms in the expansion of
x
.

By ignoring the remainder terms from the expansions (
3.2
) and (
3.1
), we can derive a stochastic Taylor approximation for the diffusion
(x,v)
at time
t
given its value at time
s
.
However this would require us to compute or estimate the derivatives
\nabla^{2}f(x_{s}),\nabla^{3}f(x_{s})

and in applications, this may become computationally expensive (especially if
d
is high).
That said, this does indicate that “third order” methods can be implemented for ULD.
Hence we seek a numerical method that captures the Taylor expansions in Theorem
3.1
but only requires evaluations of
\nabla f
(and is therefore “derivative-free” in a certain sense).

3.2. Derivation of the shifted ODE method

Our key insight (which is really an insight from rough path theory
[
41
]
) is that SDEs are a particular example of so-called “controlled differential equations” (CDEs). In our setting, we consider the following CDE:

\displaystyle d\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{v}_{t}\,dt,

(3.9)

\displaystyle d\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\gamma\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}_{t}\,dt-u\nabla f(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}_{t})\,dt+\sigma\,dX_{t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

where
X:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
is continuous and has bounded variation so that (
3.9
) is well defined.
Moreover, by constructing the path
X
using information about the Brownian motion
W
,
we can obtain a CDE solution that approximates the SDE solution. To understand how
X
should be constructed, we first note that (
3.9
) admits a Taylor expansion analogues to (
3.1
) – (
3.2
). The only difference is that the chain rule is employed instead of Itô’s lemma.

Theorem 3.3

(
Taylor expansion of underdamped Langevin CDE
)
.

Consider the CDE (
3.9
) and suppose
f
is three times continuously differentiable. Then for
0\leq s\leq t
,

\displaystyle\overline{x}_{t}=\overline{x}_{s}

\displaystyle+\overline{x}_{s}\,h-\frac{1}{2}\big(\cdots\big)h^{2}+\sigma\int_{s}^{t}X_{s,r}\,dr-\sigma\gamma\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}X_{s,{r_{2}}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

(3.10)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}\big(\cdots\big)h^{3}+\sigma\big(\cdots\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}X_{s,r_{3}}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}-\frac{1}{24}\big(\cdots\big)h^{4}+R^{\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}}(h,\overline{x}_{s},\overline{v}_{s})\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overline{v}_{t}=\overline{v}_{s}

\displaystyle-\big(\gamma\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}_{s}+u\nabla f(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}_{s})\big)h+\sigma X_{s,t}-\sigma\gamma\int_{s}^{t}X_{s,r}\,dr+\frac{1}{2}\big(\cdots\big)h^{2}

(3.11)

\displaystyle+\sigma\big(\cdots\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}X_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}-\frac{1}{6}\big(\cdots\big)h^{3}-\sigma\gamma\big(\cdots\big)\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{s}^{r_{2}}X_{s,r_{3}}\,dr_{3}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle-\sigma u\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\nabla^{3}f(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}_{s})\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}_{s},(r_{2}-s)X_{s,r_{2}}\big)\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}+R^{\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}}(h,\overline{x}_{s},\overline{v}_{s}),

where
h=t-s
, the coefficients
(\hskip 0.7113pt\cdots)
are given in Theorem
3.1
and the remainder terms
R^{\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}}(h,\overline{x}_{s},\overline{v}_{s}),R^{\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}}(h,\overline{x}_{s},\overline{v}_{s})
have the same form as their stochastic counterparts (
3.3
), (
3.4
).

Thus, to approximate the SDE solution, we construct
X
over the interval
[s,t]
so that

\displaystyle 1.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,X_{s,t}=W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle 2.

\displaystyle\,\int_{s}^{t}X_{s,r}\,dr=\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,r}\,dr\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle 3.

\displaystyle\,\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}X_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}W_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt.

At this point, it is worth noting that the above three properties are not sufficient for accurately approximating general SDEs. This is because a general SDE will contain additional non-Gaussian iterated integrals of Brownian motion in its Taylor expansion.
The two most notable examples of such integrals (neither of which appears for ULD) are

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,r}^{(i)}\,dW_{r}^{(j)},\hskip 14.22636pt\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}W_{s,r_{2}}\,dW_{r_{2}}\,dr_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt,

for
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
with
i\neq j
. Due to the specific structure of the Langevin SDE/CDE, we do not need to take these integrals into consideration when constructing the path
X
.

To make the CDE (
3.9
) easier to discretize, we will assume that
X
is piecewise linear.
Moreover, the path should have as few pieces as possible (to reduce computational cost).
The following theorem shows that, in general, it is not enough for
X
to have two pieces.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
a\in(s,t)
and
b,c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that the piecewise linear path
X
is defined on
[s,t]
by
X_{r}:=b\big(\frac{r-s}{a-s}\big)
for
r\in[s,a]
and
X_{r}:=b+(c-b)\big(\frac{r-a}{t-a}\big)
for
r\in[a,t]
.
Then
X_{s,t}=c
,
\int_{s}^{t}X_{s,r}\,dr=\frac{1}{2}(b+c)(t-s)
and
\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}X_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}
depends linearly on
b,c
and quadratically on
\hskip 0.7113pta
. In particular, for every
b,\hskip 0.7113ptc\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is bounded as a function of
a
.

Proof.

Using integration by parts, we can express the iterated integral of
X
as

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}X_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}=(t-s)\int_{s}^{t}X_{s,r}\,dr-\int_{s}^{t}(r-s)\hskip 0.7113ptX_{s,r}\,dr\hskip 0.7113pt.

By evaluating the above, we see that the integral is linear in
b,c
and quadratic in
a
.
∎

Therefore, the path
X
should be a piecewise linear path on
[s,t]
with three pieces
and thus defined by five variables: the discretization values
b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and times
a_{1},a_{2}\in(s,t)
.
Our key observation is that we can set
a_{1}=s
,
a_{2}=t
. That is, we give
X
“vertical pieces”.
Since the Langevin equation has additive noise, it is trivial to solve the CDE along these vertical pieces and so (
3.9
) reduces to a single ODE (corresponding to the middle piece).

Figure 3.1
:
Brownian motion approximated using piecewise linear paths with vertical pieces.

Finally to derive the shifted ODE method, we determine the unknown variables
b,c,d
.
To simply calculations, we express the integrals of
W
using independent random vectors.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
W
denote a Brownian motion. With
h=t-s
, we define the integrals:

\displaystyle H_{s,t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h}\int_{s}^{t}\bigg(W_{s,r}-\frac{r-s}{h}W_{s,t}\bigg)\,dr,

(3.12)

\displaystyle K_{s,t}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h^{2}}\int_{s}^{t}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}h-(r-s)\bigg)\bigg(W_{s,r}-\frac{r-s}{h}W_{s,t}\bigg)\,dr.

(3.13)

Then
W_{s,t}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,hI_{d}\big)
,
H_{s,t}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\frac{1}{12}hI_{d}\big)
,
K_{s,t}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\frac{1}{720}hI_{d}\big)
are independent and

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}W_{s,r}\,dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}hW_{s,t}+hH_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}W_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}h^{2}W_{s,t}+\frac{1}{2}h^{2}H_{s,t}+h^{2}K_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(3.15)

Proof.

The first identity is trivial and the second identity follows by a direct calculation (involving integration by parts). For each
i\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
, we can identify
W_{s,t}^{(i)},H_{s,t}^{(i)},K_{s,t}^{(i)}
as rescaled coefficients from the polynomial expansion of
W^{(i)}
(see Theorem 2.3 of
[
25
]
).
In particular, this means that
W_{s,t},H_{s,t},K_{s,t}
are independent centered Gaussian vectors.
The variances of both
H_{0,1}
and
K_{0,1}
can be directly computed using Fubini’s theorem and the Brownian bridge covariance function,
\mathbb{E}[B_{r_{1}}B_{r_{2}}]=\min(r_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt,r_{2})-r_{1}r_{2}
for
r_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt,r_{2}\in[0,1]
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\big(H_{0,1}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big[B_{r_{1}}B_{r_{2}}\big]\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}=\frac{1}{12}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\big(K_{0,1}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-r_{1}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-r_{2}\bigg)\mathbb{E}\big[B_{r_{1}}B_{r_{2}}\big]\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}=\frac{1}{720}\hskip 0.7113pt.

The result follows by the natural Brownian scaling
(H_{s,t}\sim\sqrt{h}H_{0,1}\hskip 0.7113pt,K_{s,t}\sim\sqrt{h}K_{0,1})
.
∎

Using the identities from Theorem
3.5
, we can derive a simple formula for the path
X
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
0\leq s<t
and
h=t-s
. We define a path
X:[s,t]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
as follows:

\displaystyle X_{r}:=\begin{cases}\big(H_{s,t}+6K_{s,t}\big)+\big(W_{s,t}-12K_{s,t}\big)\displaystyle\frac{r-s}{h}\hskip 0.7113pt,&\text{for}\,\,\,r\in(s,t)\\
\,\,W_{r}\hskip 0.7113pt,&\text{for}\,\,r\in\{s,t\}\end{cases}.

Then
\displaystyle X_{s,t}=W_{s,t}\hskip 0.7113pt,\int_{s}^{t}\hskip-2.84526ptX_{s,r}\,dr=\int_{s}^{t}\hskip-2.84526ptW_{s,r}\,dr\,
and
\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptX_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{r_{1}}\hskip-2.84526ptW_{s,r_{2}}\,dr_{2}\,dr_{1}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Proof.

The result follows by a direct calculation with the identities (
3.14
) and (
3.15
).
∎

By driving the CDE (
3.9
) with the above path, we obtain the shifted ODE method (definition
1.1
). The ODE (
1.2
) defined on
[0,1]
is then obtained by a change of variables.

3.3. Derivation of the SORT method

For simplicity, we consider the following ODE:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\hskip 0.7113ptx_{r}^{n}\\[-4.0pt]
\hskip 0.7113ptv_{r}^{n}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,v_{r}^{n}\\[-4.0pt]
\,-u\nabla f\big(x_{r}^{n}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg),

(3.16)

on
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
. Then by applying the standard third order Runge-Kutta method
[
5
]
, we have

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\frac{1}{2}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}-\frac{1}{2}u\nabla f\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}-\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+2\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}-2u\nabla f\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\frac{1}{6}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\frac{2}{3}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\frac{1}{6}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}-\frac{2}{3}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)h_{n}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\big)h_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt.

Our first observation is that
\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}
approximates the solution at the midpoint of
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
.
and thus can be improved by including the
O(h^{2})
terms from the Taylor expansion (
3.1
).
Another improvement would be to replace
\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\big)
with
\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)
in the final line. Since
\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}
does not depend on
\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{(2)}
, this makes
\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}
obsolete and thus can be removed.
Finally, we substitute for
\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}
and
\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}
in the formula of
\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}
and rearrange the terms.
After performing these improvements, the Runge-Kutta method simplifies to the following:

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\frac{1}{2}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}-\frac{1}{8}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}+\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}u\nabla f\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+\frac{1}{2}}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)h_{n}-\frac{2}{3}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+\frac{1}{2}}\big)h_{n}-\frac{1}{6}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)h_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

which coincides with the SORT method when
\gamma=\sigma=0
. If friction is included in (
3.16
), and we thus write
\frac{d}{dr}v_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}=-\gamma v_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}-u\nabla f\big(x_{r}^{n}\big)\hskip 0.7113pt
, we may apply a change of variable to give

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dr}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\hskip 0.7113ptx_{r}^{n}\\[-4.0pt]
\hskip 0.7113ptw_{r}^{n}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}e^{-\gamma r}w_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,-u\hskip 0.7113pte^{\gamma r}\hskip 0.7113pt\nabla f\big(x_{r}^{n}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg),

(3.17)

where
w_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}:=e^{\gamma r}v_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\hskip 0.7113pt
. By applying the third order Runge-Kutta method to this ODE and applying similar arguments as before, we arrive at the SORT method without noise terms.
Finally, by replacing
\nabla f(\,\cdot\,)
with
\nabla f(\,\cdot\,)+\sigma\Big(\frac{W_{n}-12K_{n}}{h_{n}}\Big)
, we can obtain the desired method.

3.4. Derivation of the SOFA method

To apply a splitting method, we express the ODE’s vector field as a sum of two operators. Due to the structure of the ODE, we write

\frac{d}{dr}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-4.0pt]
\,\overline{v}_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\underbrace{\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{v}_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}h_{n}\\[-4.0pt]
\,0\end{matrix}\Bigg)}_{=:\hskip 0.7113ptA}+\underbrace{\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-4.0pt]
\,-\gamma\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{v}_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}-u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{x}_{r}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\sigma\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\end{matrix}\Bigg)}_{=:\hskip 0.7113ptB}.

In this case, the ODEs governed by
A
and
B
admit the following closed-form solutions:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}^{A}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}x\\[-4.0pt]
v\end{matrix}\Bigg)

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}x+v\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113ptt\\[-4.0pt]
v\end{matrix}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}^{B}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}x\\[-4.0pt]
v\end{matrix}\Bigg)

\displaystyle:=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,x\\[-4.0pt]
\,e^{-\gamma\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}t}v+\Big(\hskip-0.7113pt-u\nabla f(x)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}+\sigma\big(W_{n}-12K_{n}\big)\Big)\Big(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma h_{n}t}}{\gamma\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}}\Big)\end{matrix}\Bigg).

It is important to note that these closed-form solutions are also valid when
t
is negative.
Thus, it is straightforward to apply the fourth order splitting due to Forest and Ruth
[
23
]
:

\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}_{1}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-4.0pt]
\,\overline{v}_{1}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)\approx\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}+\phi}^{B}\circ\varphi_{1+2\phi}^{A}\circ\varphi_{-\phi}^{B}\circ\varphi_{-1-4\phi}^{A}\circ\varphi_{-\phi}^{B}\circ\varphi_{1+2\phi}^{A}\circ\varphi_{\frac{1}{2}+\phi}^{B}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overline{x}_{0}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-4.0pt]
\,\overline{v}_{0}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg),

where

\phi:=\frac{-1+\sqrt[3]{2\hskip 0.7113pt}\,}{2\big(2-\sqrt[3]{2\hskip 0.7113pt}\,\big)}\hskip 0.7113pt.

By applying this splitting method to the shifted ODE, we obtain the SOFA method (definition
1.3
). In addition to its high order of convergence, the above splitting method is symplectic when used on Hamiltonian systems (which the SDE (
1.1
) becomes with
\gamma=0
).
Hence all that is required to show the SOFA method is quasi-symplectic in the sense of
[
45
]
is to check that the change in phase volume for every step does not depend on
\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)
:

\displaystyle\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(4)}\big)}\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(4)}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(3)}\big)}\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(3)}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)}\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(0)}\big)}\frac{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(0)}\big)}{D\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)}

\displaystyle=1\cdot e^{-\gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\phi)h_{n}}\cdot e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}\cdot e^{\gamma\phi h_{n}}\cdot e^{-\gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\phi)h_{n}}\cdot 1

\displaystyle=e^{-\gamma h_{n}},

where
\big\{\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(i)},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(i)}\big)\big\}
are given by definition
1.3
and we use the same notation as in
[
45
]
.
Similarly, any numerical method for ULD obtained by applying a symplectic splitting method to the shifted ODE will be quasi-symplectic. As well as the SOFA method, the Strang splitting method and OBABO scheme discussed in Section
4
are quasi-symplectic.

3.5. Error analysis of the shifted ODE method

In this section, we present our results concerning the convergence of the shifted ODE method and discuss the techniques used in our error analysis. Recall that we use
\{(\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n},\widetilde{v}_{n})\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
to denote the Markov chain generated by the shifted ODE method (at times
\{t_{n}\}
). Our main theorem is given below

Theorem 3.7

(
Convergence of the shifted ODE method
)
.

Let
\big\{(\widetilde{x}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n})\big\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
denote the approximation of the underdamped Langevin diffusion
\big\{(x_{t},v_{t})\big\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
obtained using the shifted ODE method with a fixed step size
h>0
and the same underlying Brownian motion.
We assume that the potential
f
is
m
-strongly convex and has an
M
-Lipschitz continuous gradient so that the diffusion process has a unique stationary measure
\pi
. Suppose further that
(x_{0},v_{0})\sim\pi
and both processes have the same initial velocity
\widetilde{v}_{0}=v_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,u\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\big)
. Let
h_{\max}>0
be fixed. Then for
\lambda\in[0,\frac{\gamma}{2})
there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
, independent of
h
and
d
such that for
h\leq h_{\max}
and
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\lambda^{2}+2(\gamma-\lambda)^{2}}}{\gamma-2\lambda}\,e^{-n\alpha h}\big\|\widetilde{x}_{0}-x_{0}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}+c_{1}\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{3}{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(3.18)

where the contraction rate
\alpha
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{\big((\gamma-\lambda)^{2}-uM\big)\vee\big(um-\lambda^{2}\big)}{\gamma-2\lambda}\,.

(3.19)

If we assume further that the Hessian of
f
is
M_{2}
-Lipschitz continuous, then there exists a constant
c_{2}>0
, independent of
h
and
d
such that for
h\leq h_{\max}
and
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\lambda^{2}+2(\gamma-\lambda)^{2}}}{\gamma-2\lambda}\,e^{-n\alpha h}\big\|\widetilde{x}_{0}-x_{0}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}+c_{2}\hskip 0.7113ptd\hskip 0.7113pth^{\frac{5}{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(3.20)

If we additionally assume that
f
is three times continuously differentiable and the third derivative is
M_{3}
-Lipschitz continuous, then there exists a constant
c_{3}>0
, independent of
h
and
d
such that for
h\leq h_{\max}
and
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\lambda^{2}+2(\gamma-\lambda)^{2}}}{\gamma-2\lambda}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}n\alpha h}\big\|\widetilde{x}_{0}-x_{0}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}+c_{3}\hskip 0.7113ptd^{\hskip 0.7113pt\frac{3}{2}}\hskip 0.7113pth^{3}.

(3.21)

Remark 3.8
.

These error estimates follow directly using Theorems
C.8
,
D.7
and
D.13
. In these theorems and throughout the appendix, the various constants are given explicitly.
As
W_{2}\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{\nu}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,e^{-f}\hskip 0.7113pt\big)\leq\|\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}
where
\widetilde{x}_{n}\sim\widetilde{\nu}_{n}
, this theorem gives the results in Table
1.1
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\{y_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
(see the end of Section
2
or Theorem
B.1
), we have

\displaystyle(\gamma-2\lambda)^{2}\|\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\eta-\lambda)(\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}})\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|\big((\eta\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n})-(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}})\big)-\big((\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n})-(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t_{n}}+v_{t_{n}})\big)\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\big\|(\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n})-(\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}+v_{n})\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}+2\big\|(\eta\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}+\widetilde{v}_{n})-(\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{n}+v_{n})\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\|y_{n}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt,

where we obtained the third line using Minkoski’s inequality. Since we can estimate each
\|\hskip 0.7113pt\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}
using
\|y_{n}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}
, the inequalities (
3.18
), (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) now follow immediately from Theorems
C.8
,
D.7
and
D.13
respectively.
∎

In this paper, we are primarily interested in the dependence of the
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error on both the dimension
d
and step size
h
. However, under the standard assumptions on the potential (
f
is
m
-strongly convex and
\nabla f
is
M
-Lipschitz continuous), one is additionally interested in the dependence on the conditional number
\kappa:=\frac{M}{m}
(see, for example, Table 1 in
[
52
]
).
Using the explicit formulae derived in the appendices, it follows from Corollary
C.9
that

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\hskip 0.7113pt\sigma uM\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}+\frac{\sqrt{10}+\sqrt{15}}{15}\Big)}{\big((\gamma-\lambda)^{2}-uM\big)\vee\big(um-\lambda^{2}\big)}+O\big(h_{\max}\big).

(3.22)

Thus the leading term in the error estimate depends linearly on the condition number
\kappa
,
In particular, this means the shifted ODE method matches the
\mathcal{O}\big(\kappa\sqrt{d}\hskip 0.7113pth^{1.5}\big)
convergence rate of the randomized midpoint method
[
52
]
(which was shown to be order optimal in
[
8
]
).

For the rest of the section, we shall outline our approach to deriving the estimates in Theorem
3.7
. Our full error analysis of ULD (with calculations) is given in the appendix.

Step 1. (Change of variable)
For our analysis, we rewrite the shifted ODE method:

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widetilde{x}_{n+1}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widetilde{v}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\Bigg):=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{x}_{t_{n+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widehat{v}_{t_{n+1}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)+12K_{n}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg),

where
\big\{\big(\widehat{x}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn},\widehat{v}_{t}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\big\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\in\hskip 0.7113pt[t_{n},t_{n+1}]}
solves the following ODE,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\\[-3.0pt]
\,-\gamma\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\widehat{v}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}+\sigma\big(H_{n}+6K_{n}\big)\big)-u\nabla f\big(\,\widehat{x}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big)\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)+\frac{W_{n}-12K_{n}}{h_{n}}\,\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

(3.23)

with initial condition
\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\widehat{x}_{t_{n}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn},\widehat{v}_{t_{n}}^{\hskip 0.7113ptn}\big):=\big(\widetilde{x}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)
. This form of the shifted ODE method is convenient to work with as we can easily Taylor expand (
3.23
) from the point
\big(\widetilde{x}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,\widetilde{v}_{n}\big)
.
Moreover, from the above, we see that the shifted ODE method reduces to another ODE approximation known as the “log-ODE” method (which is studied in
[
25
]
) when
K_{n}=0
.

Step 2. (2-Wasserstein contractivity)
Typically local errors propagate along the trajectory of the SDE solution so that we would not expect
\underset{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}{\sup}\hskip 0.7113pt\|\widetilde{x}_{n}-x_{t_{n}}\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}
to exist.

However such error estimates (that hold for all
n\geq 0
) can be established when the SDE exhibits 2-Wasserstein contractivity
[
39
]
. For ULD, contractivity does not hold directly for the solution process
\{(x_{t},v_{t})\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
but instead holds after introducing new coordinates (this was first observed in
[
22
]
). We use the recent approach given by
[
16
]
, which considers

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\lambda\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t}+v_{t}\\[-3.0pt]
\,\eta\hskip 0.7113ptx_{t}+v_{t}\end{matrix}\Bigg)\Bigg\}_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0},

(3.24)

where
\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2}\gamma)
and
\eta:=\gamma-\lambda\hskip 0.7113pt
, to obtain
2
-Wasserstein contractivity. However, unlike in previous works, we utilized the
2
-Wasserstein contractivity of the approximation process.
That is, using a synchronous coupling and essentially the same proof as in
[
16
]
, we establish a
2
-Wasserstein contraction property for the shifted ODE approximation (Theorem
B.1
).
Alternatively we could have used the straightforward coupling approach proposed by
[
12
]
, however this would have restricted our choice of parameters for ULD to
\gamma=2
and
u=\frac{1}{M}
.

We then follow the arguments of
[
39
]
with the slight modification that the contraction is applied to the approximation process instead of the diffusion. This is illustrated below:

Figure 3.2
:
Graph outlining the general strategy for our error analysis.

By introducing a third process (obtained by applying the shifted ODE method to the diffusion at each time
t_{n}
), we can estimate the error between the coordinate changed approximation and diffusion processes at time
t_{n+1}
in terms of the same error at time
t_{n}
.

Step 3. (Local error estimates without global bounds)
From figure
3.2
, it is clear that we need to derive error estimates between
(x_{t_{n+1}},v_{t_{n+1}})
and
(x_{t_{n+1}}^{\prime},v_{t_{n+1}}^{\prime})
.
Using the Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla f
along with the fact that both processes satisfy similar differential equations, it will be straightforward to derive
\mathbb{L}_{p}
estimates that are
O\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 0.7113pth^{2.5}\big)
(Theorem
C.5
). Using the
2
-Wasserstein contractivity of the shifted ODE approximation, this gives a global
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error estimate between
\{(x_{t_{n}},v_{t_{n}})\}
and
\{(\widetilde{x}_{n},\widetilde{v}_{n})\}
that is
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 0.7113pth^{1.5}\big)
.

Step 4. (Global
\mathbb{L}_{p}
bounds)
To derive the higher order error estimates, we
require that the diffusion and approximation processes are globally bounded (in an
\mathbb{L}_{p}
sense).
Since the underdamped Langevin diffusion is well studied, it has already been shown that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\big\|v_{t}\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{p}}\leq C(p)\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\sup_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\big\|\nabla f(x_{t})\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}\leq\sqrt{Md\hskip 0.7113pt},

(3.25)

for
p\geq 1
where
(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})\sim\pi
and
C(p)
is a positive constant depending only on
p

[
15
]
.
However, unlike in
[
16
]
, these bounds were not sufficient to derive high order estimates.
Therefore the main result in this part of our error analysis is the following global
\mathbb{L}_{4}
bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}\big\|\nabla f(x_{t})\big\|_{\mathbb{L}_{4}}\leq\sqrt[4]{3}\,\sqrt{Md\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 0.7113pt.

(3.26)

Step 5. (Local error estimates with global bounds)
Using these bounds and the Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla f
and
\nabla^{2}f
, we can estimate the local
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error as
O\big(d\hskip 0.7113pth^{3.5}\big)
.
We obtain this estimate by Taylor expanding the “
\nabla f
terms” in both the SDE and ODE.
By the same arguments as before, we arrive at a global
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error estimate that is
\mathcal{O}\big(d\hskip 0.7113pth^{2.5}\big)
.
However, as stated in Theorem
3.7
, it is possible to derive a third order
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error estimate.
To do this, we use the same strategy of proof as
[
39
]
and consider the local mean deviation:

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\mathbb{E}_{n}\Bigg[\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,x_{n+1}^{\prime}-x_{t_{n+1}}\\[-3.0pt]
\,v_{n+1}^{\prime}-v_{t_{n+1}}\end{matrix}\Bigg)\Bigg]\Bigg\|_{\mathbb{L}_{2}}\hskip 0.7113pt.

By extending our Taylor expansion of the “
\nabla f
terms” using the third derivative
\nabla^{3}f
, we can show that that the local mean deviation is
O\big(d^{1.5}h^{4}\big)
. Intuitively, this means the
O\big(h^{3.5}\big)
component of the local error is unbiased and thus propagates like a random walk.
Since random walks grow like
O(\sqrt{N}\hskip 0.7113pt)
, this leads to a global
\mathbb{L}_{2}
error estimate of
\mathcal{O}\big(h^{3}\big)
.
To apply this argument rigorously, we follow the proof of Theorem 1 in
[
39
]
and obtain the desired
\mathcal{O}\big(d^{1.5}h^{3}\big)
estimate. Due to some technical details, this error estimate only has half of the
2
-Wasserstein contraction rate as the
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}\hskip 0.7113pth^{1.5}\big)
and
\mathcal{O}\big(d\hskip 0.7113pth^{2.5}\big)
estimates.

4
Related derivative-free numerical methods for ULD

In this section, we will survey some of the numerical methods recently proposed for ULD.
Before we discuss methods specifically designed for Langevin dynamics, we first note a connection between the shifted ODE method and the “log-ODE” method studied in
[
25
]
.

Definition 4.1

(
Log-ODE method for ULD
)
.

When applied to SDE (
1.1
), each step of the log-ODE method (Definition 3.12 in
[
25
]
) can be written as
\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big):=\big(Q_{1}^{n},P_{1}^{n}\big)

where
\big\{\big(Q_{t}^{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,P_{t}^{n}\big)\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
solves the following ODE:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\hskip 0.7113ptQ^{n}\\[-3.0pt]
\hskip 0.7113ptP^{n}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,P^{n}\\[-3.0pt]
\,-\gamma P^{n}-u\nabla f\big(Q^{n}\big)\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)h_{n}+\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
\,\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)W_{n}+\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\sigma\hskip 0.7113pt\\[-3.0pt]
\,-\gamma\sigma\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)h_{n}H_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

(4.1)

with initial condition
\big(Q_{0}^{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,P_{0}^{n}\big):=\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\hskip 0.7113pt,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)
.

Using the change of variables discussed in the previous section, it follows that the log-ODE (
4.1
) can be obtained from the shifted ODE (
3.23
) simply by setting
K_{n}=0
.
Since the log-ODE method does not use the random vectors
\{K_{n}\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}
, it cannot match as many terms in the stochastic Taylor expansion of ULD as the shifted ODE method.
So although we expect the log-ODE method to achieve a second order convergence rate when applied to ULD, it is likely to be inferior to the higher order shifted ODE method.

We shall now discuss four different methods for the underdamped Langevin diffusion.

Definition 4.2

(
Strang splitting

[
3
]
)
.

We construct a numerical solution

\big\{\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)\big\}_{n\hskip 0.7113pt\geq\hskip 0.7113pt0}

for the SDE (
1.1
) by setting
\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526pt0}\big):=(x_{0}\hskip 0.7113pt,v_{0})

and for
n\geq 0
, defining
\big(\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1},\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)
as

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}-\frac{1}{2}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\\[-1.0pt]
\,\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}\end{matrix}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\\[-1.0pt]
\,e^{-\gamma h_{n}}\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)+\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,\Big(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma h_{n}}}{\gamma}\Big)\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(1)}\\[-1.0pt]
\,0\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)+\sigma\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\hskip-4.2679pt\int_{t_{n}}^{t}e^{-\gamma(t-s)}dW_{s}\hskip 0.7113ptdt\\[-1.0pt]
\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}e^{-\gamma(t_{n+1}-t)}dW_{t}\end{matrix}\,\Bigg)\hskip 0.7113pt,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}

\displaystyle:=\overrightarrow{v\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}^{\hskip 0.7113pt(2)}-\frac{1}{2}u\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn+1}\big)\hskip 0.7113pth_{n}\hskip 0.7113pt.

The above splitting method is obtained by expressing the SDE (
1.1
) as the sum of two subsystems but unlike in the derivation of the SOFA method, we split the SDE as follows:

d\Bigg(\begin{matrix}x_{t}\\[-3.0pt]
v_{t}\end{matrix}\Bigg)=\underbrace{\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,v_{t}\,dt\\[-3.0pt]
-\gamma\hskip 0.7113ptv_{t}\,dt+\sigma\,dW_{t}\end{matrix}\Bigg)}_{=:\hskip 0.7113ptA}+\underbrace{\Bigg(\begin{matrix}\,0\\[-3.0pt]
-u\nabla f(x_{t})\,dt\end{matrix}\Bigg)}_{=:\hskip 0.7113ptB}.

The subsystem
A
corresponds to a physical Brownian motion
[
26
]
and thus can be sampled exactly. The subsystem
B
can be solved simply by evaluating the gradient
\nabla f
.
The stochastic integrals which appear in definition
4.2
follow a (joint) normal distribution.

Theorem 4.3

(Distribution of integrals required to simulate physical Brownian motion)
.

Let
0\leq s\leq t
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\int_{s}^{t}e^{-\gamma(t-\tau)}dW_{\tau}\\
\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{\tau}e^{-\gamma(\tau-r)}dW_{r}\,d\tau\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\Bigg(\begin{matrix}0\\[-3.0pt]
0\end{matrix}\Bigg)\,,\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1-e^{-2\gamma h}}{2\gamma}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}&\frac{(1-e^{-\gamma h})^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\\[3.0pt]
\frac{(1-e^{-\gamma h})^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}&\frac{4e^{-\gamma h}-e^{-2\gamma h}+2\gamma h-3}{2\gamma^{3}}\hskip 0.7113ptI_{d}\end{pmatrix}\right),

where
h:=t-s
.

We refer the reader to Appendix
E
for additional details on the stochastic integrals used by numerical methods for ULD. We note that the second integral is often written as

\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{\tau}e^{-\gamma(\tau-r)}dW_{r}\,d\tau=\int_{s}^{t}\bigg(\frac{1-e^{-\gamma(t-r)}}{\gamma}\bigg)\,dW_{r}\hskip 0.7113pt,

by Fubini’s theorem. We prefer the first form as it is clearer how it comes from SDE
A
.

By considering the stochastic Taylor expansion of the above Strang splitting, we would expect the method to achieve an
\mathbb{L}_{2}
convergence rate of
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{d\hskip 0.7113pt}h\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
when
\nabla f
is
M
-Lipschitz continuous and
\mathcal{O}\big(d\hskip 0.7113pth^{2}\hskip 0.7113pt\big)
if, in addition,
\nabla^{2}f
is also assumed to be Lipschitz continuous. This conjecture is supported by our numerical experiment, where the Strang splitting exhibited a second order rate of convergence. Another particularly attractive feature of this numerical method is that it only requires one additional evaluation of
\nabla f
per step (since, just as for our methods,
\nabla f\big(\hskip 0.7113pt\overrightarrow{x\,}_{\hskip-2.84526ptn}\big)
can be computed during the previous step).
Whilst this Strang splitting does not appear to be extensively studied in the literature,
we believe that it offers an attractive compromise between accuracy and computational cost.

As previously stated, the splitting used to derive the SOFA method differs from the one used to derive the above Strang splitting. In some sense, the second splitting is more desirable as the associated numerical methods reduce to the exact solution when
\nabla f=0
.
